for the pure-computation and concavity questions (showing where the curve is concave up/down), and the
curve with its \textbf{stationary points} marked and classified (max / min / point of inflection) for the
second-derivative-test and optimisation questions.

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 01\quad\small\texttt{(d3-001)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 01 (d3-001)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Find \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} \) when \( y = x^4 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Differentiate once to find \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 4x^3
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule: multiply by the index and reduce it by 1. The first derivative measures the gradient of the curve.

\textit{Step 2: Differentiate again to find \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 12x^2
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \(4x^3\): multiply \(4\) by \(3\) and reduce the index to \(2\). The second derivative is the rate of change of the gradient --- it tells you how the gradient itself is changing as \(x\) increases.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 12x^2 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 02\quad\small\texttt{(d3-002)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 02 (d3-002)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Find \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} \) when \( y = 5x^3 - 2x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 15x^2 - 2
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate each term: \(5x^3 \to 15x^2\) and \(-2x \to -2\).

\textit{Step 2: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 30x
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \(15x^2 - 2\): the constant \(-2\) vanishes.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 30x \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\begin{center}
\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth \linewidth\else \width\fi}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[>={Stealth[length=2mm]},font=\footnotesize,line cap=round,line join=round]
\begin{scope}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 03\quad\small\texttt{(d3-003)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 03 (d3-003)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 2}

\question{Find \( f''(x) \) when \( f(x) = 3x^4 - 6x^2 + 1 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find \( f'(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 12x^3 - 12x
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate each term.

\textit{Step 2: Find \( f''(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(x) = 36x^2 - 12
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \(12x^3 - 12x\): the constant \(1\) in the original has already been lost.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f''(x) = 36x^2 - 12 \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 04\quad\small\texttt{(d3-004)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 04 (d3-004)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} \) when \( y = 4x^5 - 3x^3 + 7x - 2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 20x^4 - 9x^2 + 7
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate each term; the constant \(-2\) disappears.

\textit{Step 2: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 80x^3 - 18x
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \(20x^4 - 9x^2 + 7\); the constant \(7\) disappears.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 80x^3 - 18x \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 05\quad\small\texttt{(d3-005)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 05 (d3-005)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find \( f''(x) \) when \( f(x) = x^{-2} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find \( f'(x) \) using the power rule.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = -2x^{-3}
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply by the index \(-2\) and reduce the index to \(-3\).

\textit{Step 2: Find \( f''(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(x) = (-2)(-3)x^{-4} = 6x^{-4}
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \(-2x^{-3}\): two negatives multiply to give a positive.

\textit{Step 3: Write in fraction form.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(x) = \frac{6}{x^4}
\end{gathered}\]
\( x^{-4} = \dfrac{1}{x^4} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f''(x) = \dfrac{6}{x^4} \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 06\quad\small\texttt{(d3-006)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 06 (d3-006)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} \) when \( y = \sqrt{x} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rewrite \( \sqrt{x} \) as \( x^{1/2} \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = x^{1/2}
\end{gathered}\]
Always convert surds to fractional powers before differentiating.

\textit{Step 2: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = \tfrac{1}{2}x^{-1/2}
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule: multiply by \( \tfrac{1}{2} \) and reduce index to \( -\tfrac{1}{2} \).

\textit{Step 3: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = \tfrac{1}{2} \cdot \left(-\tfrac{1}{2}\right) x^{-3/2} = -\tfrac{1}{4}x^{-3/2}
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \( \tfrac{1}{2}x^{-1/2} \): reduce the index to \( -\tfrac{3}{2} \).

\textit{Step 4: Rewrite.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = -\frac{1}{4x^{3/2}}
\end{gathered}\]
\( x^{-3/2} = \dfrac{1}{x^{3/2}} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = -\dfrac{1}{4x^{3/2}} \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 07\quad\small\texttt{(d3-007)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 07 (d3-007)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} \) when \( y = 6x^2 - \dfrac{1}{x} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rewrite using index notation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 6x^2 - x^{-1}
\end{gathered}\]
\( \dfrac{1}{x} = x^{-1} \).

\textit{Step 2: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 12x + x^{-2}
\end{gathered}\]
Derivative of \(6x^2\) is \(12x\); derivative of \(-x^{-1}\) is \(+x^{-2}\).

\textit{Step 3: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 12 - 2x^{-3} = 12 - \frac{2}{x^3}
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \(12x + x^{-2}\): derivative of \(x^{-2}\) is \(-2x^{-3}\).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 12 - \dfrac{2}{x^3} \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 08\quad\small\texttt{(d3-008)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 08 (d3-008)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Given \( f(x) = 2x^3 - 9x^2 + 12x - 5 \), find \( f''(x) \) and hence find the value of \( x \) where \( f''(x) = 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find \( f'(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 6x^2 - 18x + 12
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate term by term.

\textit{Step 2: Find \( f''(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(x) = 12x - 18
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \(6x^2 - 18x + 12\); the constant vanishes.

\textit{Step 3: Set \( f''(x) = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
12x - 18 = 0 \implies x = \frac{18}{12} = \frac{3}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Solve the linear equation for \( x \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f''(x) = 12x - 18 \), so \(\displaystyle  f''(x) = 0 \) when \(\displaystyle  x = \dfrac{3}{2} \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 09\quad\small\texttt{(d3-009)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 09 (d3-009)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} \) when \( y = (x + 2)(x - 3) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Expand the brackets.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = x^2 - x - 6
\end{gathered}\]
\( (x+2)(x-3) = x^2 - 3x + 2x - 6 \).

\textit{Step 2: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 2x - 1
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \(x^2 - x - 6\).

\textit{Step 3: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiating a linear function gives a constant.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 2 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 10\quad\small\texttt{(d3-010)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 10 (d3-010)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the second derivative of \( y = \dfrac{x^4 - 2x^2}{x} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Simplify by dividing each term by \( x \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = x^3 - 2x
\end{gathered}\]
\( \dfrac{x^4}{x} = x^3 \) and \( \dfrac{-2x^2}{x} = -2x \).

\textit{Step 2: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 3x^2 - 2
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule applied to each term.

\textit{Step 3: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6x
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \(3x^2 - 2\); the constant disappears.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6x \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 11\quad\small\texttt{(d3-011)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 11 (d3-011)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find the value of \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} \) when \( x = 2 \), given that \( y = x^3 - 5x \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 3x^2 - 5
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule applied term by term.

\textit{Step 2: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6x
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \(3x^2 - 5\).

\textit{Step 3: Substitute \( x = 2 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2}\bigg|_{x=2} = 6(2) = 12
\end{gathered}\]
Replace \(x\) with \(2\) in the second derivative expression.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 12 \) when \(\displaystyle  x = 2 \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 12\quad\small\texttt{(d3-012)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 12 (d3-012)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Given \( f(x) = x^4 - 8x^2 \), find \( f''(3) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find \( f'(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 4x^3 - 16x
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate each term.

\textit{Step 2: Find \( f''(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(x) = 12x^2 - 16
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \(4x^3 - 16x\).

\textit{Step 3: Substitute \( x = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(3) = 12(9) - 16 = 108 - 16 = 92
\end{gathered}\]
\( 3^2 = 9 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f''(3) = 92 \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 13\quad\small\texttt{(d3-013)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 13 (d3-013)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} \) when \( x = -1 \), given \( y = 2x^4 - x^3 + 5 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 8x^3 - 3x^2
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate term by term; the constant \(5\) vanishes.

\textit{Step 2: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 24x^2 - 6x
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \(8x^3 - 3x^2\).

\textit{Step 3: Substitute \( x = -1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
24(-1)^2 - 6(-1) = 24 + 6 = 30
\end{gathered}\]
\( (-1)^2 = 1 \) and \( -6 \times (-1) = +6 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 30 \) when \(\displaystyle  x = -1 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 14\quad\small\texttt{(d3-014)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 14 (d3-014)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{For the curve \( y = x^3 - 6x^2 + 9x + 1 \), find the coordinates of the stationary points and use the second derivative test to determine their nature.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find the first derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 3x^2 - 12x + 9
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule applied to each term.

\textit{Step 2: Set \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 0 \) and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x^2 - 12x + 9 = 0 \implies x^2 - 4x + 3 = 0 \implies (x-1)(x-3) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by 3, then factorise.

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( y \)-coordinates.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x=1: y = 1 - 6 + 9 + 1 = 5; \quad x=3: y = 27 - 54 + 27 + 1 = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute each \( x \) into the original equation.

\textit{Step 4: Find the second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6x - 12
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate the first derivative.

\textit{Step 5: Apply the second derivative test.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x=1: \frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6(1) - 12 = -6 < 0 \Rightarrow \text{maximum} \newline x=3: \frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6(3) - 12 = 6 > 0 \Rightarrow \text{minimum}
\end{gathered}\]
The sign of \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} \) tells us about concavity: \(< 0\) means concave down (local maximum), \(> 0\) means concave up (local minimum). If it were \(= 0\) the test would be inconclusive and we would need to inspect the gradient either side.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Local maximum at \(\displaystyle  (1, 5) \); local minimum at \(\displaystyle  (3, 1) \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 15\quad\small\texttt{(d3-015)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 15 (d3-015)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the stationary point of \( y = x^2 - 8x + 3 \) and use the second derivative to determine its nature.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 2x - 8
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Set equal to 0.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x - 8 = 0 \implies x = 4
\end{gathered}\]
Solve the linear equation.

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( y \)-coordinate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 16 - 32 + 3 = -13
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 4 \) into the original curve.

\textit{Step 4: Second derivative test.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 2 > 0
\end{gathered}\]
The second derivative is a constant \(+2\). A positive value means the curve is concave up everywhere, so the stationary point must be a local minimum.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Local minimum at \(\displaystyle  (4, -13) \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 16\quad\small\texttt{(d3-016)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 16 (d3-016)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Find the stationary points of \( y = 2x^3 - 3x^2 - 12x + 5 \) and determine their nature using the second derivative test.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 6x^2 - 6x - 12
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate term by term.

\textit{Step 2: Solve \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
6x^2 - 6x - 12 = 0 \implies x^2 - x - 2 = 0 \implies (x-2)(x+1) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by 6, then factorise.

\textit{Step 3: Find \( y \)-coordinates at \( x = 2 \) and \( x = -1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x=2: y = 16 - 12 - 24 + 5 = -15; \quad x=-1: y = -2 - 3 + 12 + 5 = 12
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute each \( x \) back into \( y \).

\textit{Step 4: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 12x - 6
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate the first derivative.

\textit{Step 5: Test each point.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x=2: 12(2)-6 = 18 > 0 \Rightarrow \text{minimum} \newline x=-1: 12(-1)-6 = -18 < 0 \Rightarrow \text{maximum}
\end{gathered}\]
A positive second derivative means concave up (local minimum); a negative one means concave down (local maximum). Watch the sign carefully when substituting negative \(x\)-values.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Local minimum at \(\displaystyle  (2, -15) \); local maximum at \(\displaystyle  (-1, 12) \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 17\quad\small\texttt{(d3-017)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 17 (d3-017)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Show that the curve \( y = x^3 \) has a stationary point of inflection at the origin.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find the first derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 3x^2
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Stationary points: set \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x^2 = 0 \implies x = 0
\end{gathered}\]
There is exactly one stationary point at \( x = 0 \), which gives \( y = 0 \).

\textit{Step 3: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6x
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \(3x^2\).

\textit{Step 4: Evaluate the second derivative at \( x = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2}\bigg|_{x=0} = 0
\end{gathered}\]
The second derivative is zero, so the test is inconclusive. Check behaviour either side: for \(x < 0\), \( \frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 3x^2 > 0 \); for \( x > 0 \), \( \frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} > 0 \). The gradient does not change sign, confirming a point of inflection.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Stationary point of inflection at \(\displaystyle  (0, 0) \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\begin{center}
\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth \linewidth\else \width\fi}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[>={Stealth[length=2mm]},font=\footnotesize,line cap=round,line join=round]
\begin{scope}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 18\quad\small\texttt{(d3-018)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 18 (d3-018)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the stationary point of \( y = -x^2 + 6x - 4 \) and determine its nature.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = -2x + 6
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \(-x^2 + 6x - 4\).

\textit{Step 2: Set equal to 0.}
\[\begin{gathered}
-2x + 6 = 0 \implies x = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Solve for \( x \).

\textit{Step 3: Find the \( y \)-coordinate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = -(9) + 18 - 4 = 5
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 3 \) into the original.

\textit{Step 4: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = -2 < 0
\end{gathered}\]
The second derivative is a constant \(-2\). A negative value means the curve is concave down everywhere, so the stationary point is a local maximum.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Local maximum at \(\displaystyle  (3, 5) \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 19\quad\small\texttt{(d3-019)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 19 (d3-019)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Find and classify the stationary points of \( f(x) = x^4 - 4x^2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find \( f'(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 4x^3 - 8x
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Solve \( f'(x) = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
4x(x^2 - 2) = 0 \implies x = 0,\; x = \sqrt{2},\; x = -\sqrt{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Factorise: \(4x^3 - 8x = 4x(x^2-2) = 0\).

\textit{Step 3: Find \( y \)-values.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(0)=0; \quad f(\sqrt{2}) = 4 - 8 = -4; \quad f(-\sqrt{2}) = -4
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute each \( x \) into \( f(x) = x^4 - 4x^2 \).

\textit{Step 4: Find \( f''(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(x) = 12x^2 - 8
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \(4x^3 - 8x\).

\textit{Step 5: Apply the test.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(0) = -8 < 0 \Rightarrow \text{maximum} \newline f''(\pm\sqrt{2}) = 12(2) - 8 = 16 > 0 \Rightarrow \text{minima}
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute each \( x \) into \( f''(x) \). Negative \(\Rightarrow\) concave down (max); positive \(\Rightarrow\) concave up (min). Note \( (\pm\sqrt{2})^2 = 2 \) --- squaring kills the sign, so both \(\pm\sqrt{2}\) give the same second-derivative value.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Local maximum at \(\displaystyle  (0, 0) \); local minima at \(\displaystyle  (\sqrt{2}, -4) \) and \(\displaystyle  (-\sqrt{2}, -4) \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 20\quad\small\texttt{(d3-020)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 20 (d3-020)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the second derivative of \( y = 3x^{1/3} \) and write your answer without negative or fractional indices.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 3 \cdot \tfrac{1}{3} x^{-2/3} = x^{-2/3}
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply \(3\) by \(\tfrac{1}{3}\) and reduce the index.

\textit{Step 2: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = -\tfrac{2}{3}x^{-5/3}
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply \(1\) by \(-\tfrac{2}{3}\) and reduce the index from \(-\tfrac{2}{3}\) to \(-\tfrac{5}{3}\).

\textit{Step 3: Write without fractional or negative indices.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = -\frac{2}{3x^{5/3}} = -\frac{2}{3\sqrt[3]{x^5}}
\end{gathered}\]
\( x^{5/3} = \sqrt[3]{x^5} \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = -\dfrac{2}{3\sqrt[3]{x^5}} \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 21\quad\small\texttt{(d3-021)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 21 (d3-021)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find the second derivative of \( y = x(x^2 - 5) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Expand.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = x^3 - 5x
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply \( x \) through the bracket.

\textit{Step 2: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 3x^2 - 5
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule.

\textit{Step 3: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6x
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \(3x^2 - 5\).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6x \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 22\quad\small\texttt{(d3-022)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 22 (d3-022)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the second derivative of \( f(x) = \dfrac{x^5 + 3x^3}{x^2} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Simplify the fraction.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(x) = x^3 + 3x
\end{gathered}\]
Divide each term in the numerator by \( x^2 \).

\textit{Step 2: Find \( f'(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 3x^2 + 3
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate term by term.

\textit{Step 3: Find \( f''(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(x) = 6x
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \(3x^2 + 3\); the constant vanishes.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f''(x) = 6x \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 23\quad\small\texttt{(d3-023)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 23 (d3-023)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = x^3 - 12x \). Find the coordinates of both stationary points. Use the second derivative to determine whether each is a maximum or minimum.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 3x^2 - 12
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Solve \( 3x^2 - 12 = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x^2 = 4 \implies x = \pm 2
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by 3 and take square roots.

\textit{Step 3: Find \( y \)-coordinates.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x=2: y = 8 - 24 = -16; \quad x=-2: y = -8 + 24 = 16
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute into \( y = x^3 - 12x \).

\textit{Step 4: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6x
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate the first derivative.

\textit{Step 5: Second derivative test.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x=2: 6(2) = 12 > 0 \Rightarrow \text{minimum} \newline x=-2: 6(-2) = -12 < 0 \Rightarrow \text{maximum}
\end{gathered}\]
Positive second derivative \(\Rightarrow\) concave up (local minimum); negative \(\Rightarrow\) concave down (local maximum).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Local minimum at \(\displaystyle  (2, -16) \); local maximum at \(\displaystyle  (-2, 16) \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 24\quad\small\texttt{(d3-024)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 24 (d3-024)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A function is defined as \( f(x) = x^3 + px^2 + q \), where \( p \) and \( q \) are constants. Given that \( f''(2) = 0 \), find the value of \( p \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Find \( f'(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 3x^2 + 2px
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate; \( q \) is a constant so its derivative is 0.

\textit{Step 2: Find \( f''(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(x) = 6x + 2p
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate the first derivative; note \(q\) plays no role here.

\textit{Step 3: Substitute \( x = 2 \) and set equal to 0.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(2) = 12 + 2p = 0 \implies p = -6
\end{gathered}\]
Solve the linear equation for \( p \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  p = -6 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 25\quad\small\texttt{(d3-025)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 25 (d3-025)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{A curve has equation \( y = ax^3 + bx \). Given that \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 0 \) at \( x = 1 \) and \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = -6 \) at \( x = 1 \), find the values of \( a \) and \( b \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 3ax^2 + b
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \( y = ax^3 + bx \).

\textit{Step 2: Use \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 0 \) at \( x = 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
3a + b = 0 \quad \cdots (1)
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 1 \).

\textit{Step 3: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6ax
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate the first derivative.

\textit{Step 4: Use \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = -6 \) at \( x = 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
6a = -6 \implies a = -1 \quad \cdots (2)
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 1 \).

\textit{Step 5: Back-substitute \( a = -1 \) into equation (1).}
\[\begin{gathered}
3(-1) + b = 0 \implies b = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Solve for \( b \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  a = -1,\; b = 3 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 26\quad\small\texttt{(d3-026)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 26 (d3-026)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{For \( y = x^4 - 2x^3 \), find both stationary points and determine their nature.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 4x^3 - 6x^2
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Solve \( 4x^3 - 6x^2 = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x^2(2x - 3) = 0 \implies x = 0 \text{ or } x = \tfrac{3}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Factorise by taking out \( 2x^2 \).

\textit{Step 3: Find \( y \)-values.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x=0: y=0; \quad x=\tfrac{3}{2}: y = \tfrac{81}{16} - 2 \cdot \tfrac{27}{8} = \tfrac{81}{16} - \tfrac{54}{8} = \tfrac{81}{16} - \tfrac{108}{16} = -\tfrac{27}{16}
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute each \( x \) into \( y = x^4 - 2x^3 \).

\textit{Step 4: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 12x^2 - 12x
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \(4x^3 - 6x^2\).

\textit{Step 5: Apply the test.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x=0: 0 - 0 = 0 \Rightarrow \text{inconclusive (point of inflection)} \newline x=\tfrac{3}{2}: 12(\tfrac{9}{4}) - 12(\tfrac{3}{2}) = 27 - 18 = 9 > 0 \Rightarrow \text{minimum}
\end{gathered}\]
When \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 0 \) the second-derivative test gives no information --- you must fall back on checking the sign of \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} \) either side of the point. Here the gradient is negative on both sides of \(x=0\), so it does not change sign: this is a stationary point of inflection, not a turning point.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Point of inflection at \(\displaystyle  (0, 0) \); local minimum at \(\displaystyle  \left(\dfrac{3}{2}, -\dfrac{27}{16}\right) \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 27\quad\small\texttt{(d3-027)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 27 (d3-027)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Verify that \( y = 4x^3 - 3x^4 \) has a local maximum at \( x = 1 \) by finding \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} \) and evaluating it there.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 12x^2 - 12x^3
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate term by term.

\textit{Step 2: Check \( x = 1 \) is indeed a stationary point.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x}\bigg|_{x=1} = 12 - 12 = 0 \checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
A stationary point requires \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 0 \).

\textit{Step 3: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 24x - 36x^2
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \(12x^2 - 12x^3\).

\textit{Step 4: Evaluate at \( x = 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2}\bigg|_{x=1} = 24 - 36 = -12 < 0
\end{gathered}\]
A negative second derivative at the stationary point means the curve is concave down there, confirming a local maximum.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = -12 < 0 \) at \(\displaystyle  x = 1 \), confirming a local maximum
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 28\quad\small\texttt{(d3-028)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 28 (d3-028)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A function is given by \( f(x) = 2x^3 - 3x^2 - 36x + 10 \). (a) Find \( f''(x) \). (b) Find the value(s) of \( x \) for which \( f''(x) < 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Find \( f'(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 6x^2 - 6x - 36
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate term by term.

\textit{Step 2: Find \( f''(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(x) = 12x - 6
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate the first derivative.

\textit{Step 3: (b) Solve \( f''(x) < 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
12x - 6 < 0 \implies 12x < 6 \implies x < \tfrac{1}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Solve the linear inequality.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  f''(x) = 12x - 6 \) \\[3pt]  (b) \(\displaystyle  x < \dfrac{1}{2} \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 29\quad\small\texttt{(d3-029)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 29 (d3-029)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find and classify the stationary point of \( y = x^2 + 4x - 7 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 2x + 4
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate term by term using the power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Set to 0.}
\[\begin{gathered}
2x + 4 = 0 \implies x = -2
\end{gathered}\]
Stationary points occur where the gradient is zero.

\textit{Step 3: Find \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 4 - 8 - 7 = -11
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = -2 \) into the original equation to get the \(y\)-coordinate.

\textit{Step 4: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 2 > 0
\end{gathered}\]
The second derivative is a constant \(+2\). A positive second derivative means the curve is concave up at the stationary point, so it is a local minimum.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Local minimum at \(\displaystyle  (-2, -11) \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 30\quad\small\texttt{(d3-030)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 30 (d3-030)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Given \( y = 5x^2 - x^3 \), find the range of values of \( x \) for which the curve is concave (i.e. \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} < 0 \)).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 10x - 3x^2
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 10 - 6x
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate the first derivative.

\textit{Step 3: Solve the inequality.}
\[\begin{gathered}
10 - 6x < 0 \implies 6x > 10 \implies x > \tfrac{5}{3}
\end{gathered}\]
A curve is concave (concave down) precisely where \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} < 0 \). Rearrange the inequality --- no sign flip is needed because we divide by a positive number.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x > \dfrac{5}{3} \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 31\quad\small\texttt{(d3-031)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 31 (d3-031)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 8}

\question{A curve \( C \) has equation \( y = x^3 - 3x^2 - 9x + 5 \). (a) Find \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} \) and \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} \). (b) Find the coordinates of the stationary points of \( C \). (c) Determine the nature of each stationary point.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Differentiate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 3x^2 - 6x - 9
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule applied term by term.

\textit{Step 2: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6x - 6
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate the first derivative.

\textit{Step 3: (b) Set \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x^2 - 6x - 9 = 0 \implies x^2 - 2x - 3 = 0 \implies (x-3)(x+1) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by 3, then factorise.

\textit{Step 4: Find \( y \)-coordinates.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x=3: y = 27 - 27 - 27 + 5 = -22 \newline x=-1: y = -1 - 3 + 9 + 5 = 10
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute into the original equation.

\textit{Step 5: (c) Apply second derivative test.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x=3: 6(3)-6 = 12 > 0 \Rightarrow \text{minimum} \newline x=-1: 6(-1)-6 = -12 < 0 \Rightarrow \text{maximum}
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute each \(x\) into \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6x - 6 \). Positive \(\Rightarrow\) concave up (minimum); negative \(\Rightarrow\) concave down (maximum).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 3x^2-6x-9 \), \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6x-6 \) \\[3pt]  (b) \(\displaystyle  (3, -22) \) and \(\displaystyle  (-1, 10) \) \\[3pt]  (c) Local minimum at \(\displaystyle  (3,-22) \); local maximum at \(\displaystyle  (-1,10) \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 32\quad\small\texttt{(d3-032)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 32 (d3-032)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 8}

\question{A curve has equation \( y = x^4 - 8x^2 + 3 \). (a) Find all stationary points. (b) Use the second derivative to classify each one. (c) State the coordinates of the global minimum for \( x \geq 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 4x^3 - 16x
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Solve \( 4x^3 - 16x = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
4x(x^2 - 4) = 0 \implies x = 0,\; x = 2,\; x = -2
\end{gathered}\]
Factorise \( 4x(x-2)(x+2) = 0 \).

\textit{Step 3: Find \( y \)-values.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y(0) = 3; \quad y(2) = 16 - 32 + 3 = -13; \quad y(-2) = -13
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute into \( y = x^4 - 8x^2 + 3 \).

\textit{Step 4: (b) Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\frac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 12x^2 - 16
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate the first derivative.

\textit{Step 5: Classify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x=0: 0-16 = -16 < 0 \Rightarrow \text{maximum} \newline x=\pm 2: 48-16 = 32 > 0 \Rightarrow \text{minima}
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the second derivative test at each stationary point: \(< 0\) means concave down (maximum), \(> 0\) means concave up (minimum). Note \( (\pm 2)^2 = 4 \), so both endpoints share the same value.

\textit{Step 6: (c) Global minimum for \( x \geq 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 2,\; y = -13
\end{gathered}\]
The only minimum for \( x \geq 0 \) occurs at \( x = 2 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) Stationary points at \(\displaystyle  (0,3) \), \(\displaystyle  (2,-13) \), \(\displaystyle  (-2,-13) \) \\[3pt]  (b) Maximum at \(\displaystyle  (0,3) \); minima at \(\displaystyle  (2,-13) \) and \(\displaystyle  (-2,-13) \) \\[3pt]  (c) Global minimum for \(\displaystyle  x \geq 0 \) at \(\displaystyle  (2,-13) \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 33\quad\small\texttt{(d3-033)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 33 (d3-033)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{The function \( f(x) = \dfrac{k}{x^2} + 4x \), where \( k \) is a positive constant, has a stationary point at \( x = 2 \). (a) Show that \( k = 16 \). (b) Use the second derivative to confirm that this stationary point is a minimum.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rewrite using index notation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(x) = kx^{-2} + 4x
\end{gathered}\]
\( \dfrac{k}{x^2} = kx^{-2} \).

\textit{Step 2: (a) Find \( f'(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = -2kx^{-3} + 4
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule.

\textit{Step 3: Set \( f'(2) = 0 \) and solve for \( k \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
-2k(2)^{-3} + 4 = 0 \implies -\tfrac{2k}{8} + 4 = 0 \implies \tfrac{k}{4} = 4 \implies k = 16
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 2 \), using \( (2)^{-3} = \tfrac{1}{8} \). A stationary point requires the first derivative to vanish, which fixes \( k \).

\textit{Step 4: (b) Find \( f''(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(x) = 6kx^{-4}
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \( -2kx^{-3} + 4 \): the power rule gives \( -2k \cdot (-3) x^{-4} = 6kx^{-4} \); the constant \(4\) vanishes.

\textit{Step 5: Evaluate \( f''(2) \) with \( k = 16 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(2) = 6(16)(2)^{-4} = 96 \cdot \tfrac{1}{16} = 6 > 0
\end{gathered}\]
The second derivative measures the rate of change of the gradient. Since \( f''(2) > 0 \), the curve is concave up at \( x = 2 \), so the stationary point is a local minimum.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  k = 16 \); \(\displaystyle  f''(2) = 6 > 0 \), confirming a local minimum.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 34\quad\small\texttt{(d3-034)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 34 (d3-034)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 10}

\question{A curve \( C \) has equation \( y = f(x) \), where \( f(x) = 2x^3 + 3x^2 - 12x + 1 \). (a) Find \( f'(x) \) and \( f''(x) \). (b) Find the coordinates of the stationary points of \( C \). (c) Determine the nature of each stationary point using the second derivative. (d) Find the range of values of \( x \) for which \( f''(x) > 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Find \( f'(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 6x^2 + 6x - 12
\end{gathered}\]
Power rule applied term by term.

\textit{Step 2: Find \( f''(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(x) = 12x + 6
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate the first derivative.

\textit{Step 3: (b) Set \( f'(x) = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
6x^2 + 6x - 12 = 0 \implies x^2 + x - 2 = 0 \implies (x+2)(x-1) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by 6, then factorise.

\textit{Step 4: Find \( y \)-coordinates.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(1) = 2 + 3 - 12 + 1 = -6 \newline f(-2) = -16 + 12 + 24 + 1 = 21
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 1 \) and \( x = -2 \) into \( f(x) \).

\textit{Step 5: (c) Second derivative test.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(1) = 12 + 6 = 18 > 0 \Rightarrow \text{minimum} \newline f''(-2) = -24 + 6 = -18 < 0 \Rightarrow \text{maximum}
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute each \( x \) into \( f''(x) = 12x + 6 \). Positive (concave up) gives a minimum; negative (concave down) gives a maximum.

\textit{Step 6: (d) Solve \( f''(x) > 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
12x + 6 > 0 \implies x > -\tfrac{1}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Rearrange the inequality.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  f'(x) = 6x^2+6x-12 \), \(\displaystyle  f''(x) = 12x+6 \) \\[3pt]  (b) \(\displaystyle  (1,-6) \) and \(\displaystyle  (-2,21) \) \\[3pt]  (c) Minimum at \(\displaystyle  (1,-6) \); maximum at \(\displaystyle  (-2,21) \) \\[3pt]  (d) \(\displaystyle  x > -\dfrac{1}{2} \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 35\quad\small\texttt{(d3-035)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 35 (d3-035)}
\textit{Difficulty: Foundation \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 8}

\question{A manufacturer models the profit \( P \) (in thousands of pounds) from producing \( x \) units (in hundreds) as \( P(x) = -x^3 + 6x^2 + 15x \) for \( 0 \leq x \leq 8 \). (a) Find \( P'(x) \) and \( P''(x) \). (b) Find the value of \( x \) that gives a stationary point in \( (0, 8) \) and verify it is a maximum using the second derivative. (c) State the maximum profit.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Find \( P'(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
P'(x) = -3x^2 + 12x + 15
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate term by term.

\textit{Step 2: Find \( P''(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
P''(x) = -6x + 12
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate the first derivative.

\textit{Step 3: (b) Set \( P'(x) = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
-3x^2 + 12x + 15 = 0 \implies x^2 - 4x - 5 = 0 \implies (x-5)(x+1) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Divide by \(-3\) (flip signs), then factorise.

\textit{Step 4: Select the stationary point in \( (0, 8) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 5 \quad (x = -1 \text{ is outside the domain})
\end{gathered}\]
Only \( x = 5 \) lies in the interval \( 0 \leq x \leq 8 \).

\textit{Step 5: Second derivative at \( x = 5 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
P''(5) = -30 + 12 = -18 < 0
\end{gathered}\]
Negative second derivative confirms a local maximum.

\textit{Step 6: (c) Maximum profit.}
\[\begin{gathered}
P(5) = -(125) + 6(25) + 15(5) = -125 + 150 + 75 = 100
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute \( x = 5 \) into \( P(x) \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  P'(x) = -3x^2+12x+15 \), \(\displaystyle  P''(x) = -6x+12 \) \\[3pt]  (b) Maximum at \(\displaystyle  x = 5 \) (\(\displaystyle  P''(5) = -18 < 0 \)) \\[3pt]  (c) Maximum profit = \pounds 100\,000
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 36\quad\small\texttt{(d3-036)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 36 (d3-036)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} \) when \( y = \dfrac{3}{x^2} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rewrite with a negative index.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 3x^{-2}
\end{gathered}\]
Prepare for the power rule.

\textit{Step 2: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = -6x^{-3}
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 3: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 18x^{-4} = \dfrac{18}{x^4}
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate \( -6x^{-3} \) and rewrite with a positive index.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = \dfrac{18}{x^4} \)
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 37\quad\small\texttt{(d3-037)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 37 (d3-037)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find \( f''(x) \) when \( f(x) = \dfrac{4}{\sqrt{x}} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rewrite.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(x) = 4x^{-1/2}
\end{gathered}\]
\( \dfrac{1}{\sqrt{x}} = x^{-1/2} \).

\textit{Step 2: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = -2x^{-3/2}
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply by the index and reduce.

\textit{Step 3: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(x) = 3x^{-5/2} = \dfrac{3}{x^2\sqrt{x}}
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply \( -2 \) by \( -\tfrac{3}{2} \) and reduce the index.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f''(x) = \dfrac{3}{x^2\sqrt{x}} \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 38\quad\small\texttt{(d3-038)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 38 (d3-038)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} \) when \( y = 2x^3 + \dfrac{5}{x} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rewrite and differentiate once.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 2x^3 + 5x^{-1} \;\Rightarrow\; \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 6x^2 - 5x^{-2}
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule term by term.

\textit{Step 2: Differentiate again.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 12x + 10x^{-3} = 12x + \dfrac{10}{x^3}
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule to each term.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 12x + \dfrac{10}{x^3} \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 39\quad\small\texttt{(d3-039)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 39 (d3-039)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Given \( y = x^{3/2} + 4x^{1/2} \), find \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = \tfrac{3}{2}x^{1/2} + 2x^{-1/2}
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = \tfrac{3}{4}x^{-1/2} - x^{-3/2} = \dfrac{3}{4\sqrt{x}} - \dfrac{1}{x\sqrt{x}}
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate each term and simplify.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = \dfrac{3}{4\sqrt{x}} - \dfrac{1}{x\sqrt{x}} \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 40\quad\small\texttt{(d3-040)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 40 (d3-040)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the second derivative of \( f(x) = \dfrac{x^3 - 2x}{x^2} \) for \( x \neq 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Simplify first.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(x) = \dfrac{x^3}{x^2} - \dfrac{2x}{x^2} = x - 2x^{-1}
\end{gathered}\]
Divide each numerator term by \( x^2 \).

\textit{Step 2: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 1 + 2x^{-2}
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 3: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(x) = -4x^{-3} = -\dfrac{4}{x^3}
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate once more.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f''(x) = -\dfrac{4}{x^3} \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 41\quad\small\texttt{(d3-041)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 41 (d3-041)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Find \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} \) when \( y = (x + 3)^2 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Expand.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = x^2 + 6x + 9
\end{gathered}\]
\( (a+b)^2 = a^2 + 2ab + b^2 \).

\textit{Step 2: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 2x + 6
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 3: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Constant.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 2 \)
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 42\quad\small\texttt{(d3-042)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 42 (d3-042)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the second derivative of \( y = \sqrt{x}(x - 3) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Expand using indices.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = x^{3/2} - 3x^{1/2}
\end{gathered}\]
\( \sqrt{x} \cdot x = x^{3/2} \).

\textit{Step 2: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = \tfrac{3}{2}x^{1/2} - \tfrac{3}{2}x^{-1/2}
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 3: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = \tfrac{3}{4}x^{-1/2} + \tfrac{3}{4}x^{-3/2} = \dfrac{3}{4\sqrt{x}} + \dfrac{3}{4x\sqrt{x}}
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate once more and simplify.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = \dfrac{3}{4\sqrt{x}} + \dfrac{3}{4x\sqrt{x}} \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 43\quad\small\texttt{(d3-043)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 43 (d3-043)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{Given \( f(x) = x^4 - 2x^3 + 5x \), find \( f''(1) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 4x^3 - 6x^2 + 5
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(x) = 12x^2 - 12x
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate once more.

\textit{Step 3: Substitute \( x = 1 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(1) = 12 - 12 = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Evaluate.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  f''(1) = 0 \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 44\quad\small\texttt{(d3-044)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 44 (d3-044)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Given \( y = 3x^2 - 2\sqrt{x} \), find the value of \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} \) at \( x = 4 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rewrite and differentiate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = 3x^2 - 2x^{1/2} \;\Rightarrow\; \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 6x - x^{-1/2}
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6 + \tfrac{1}{2}x^{-3/2}
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate each term.

\textit{Step 3: Substitute \( x = 4 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
6 + \tfrac{1}{2}(4)^{-3/2} = 6 + \tfrac{1}{2} \cdot \tfrac{1}{8} = 6 + \tfrac{1}{16} = \tfrac{97}{16}
\end{gathered}\]
\( 4^{3/2} = (\sqrt{4})^3 = 8 \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  \left.\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2}\right|_{x=4} = \dfrac{97}{16} \)
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 45\quad\small\texttt{(d3-045)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 45 (d3-045)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A curve has equation \( y = ax^3 + bx^2 \). Given that \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 12x - 4 \), find the values of \( a \) and \( b \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Differentiate twice.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 3ax^2 + 2bx \;\Rightarrow\; \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6ax + 2b
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Compare coefficients.}
\[\begin{gathered}
6ax + 2b \equiv 12x - 4
\end{gathered}\]
Match the \( x \) term and the constant term.

\textit{Step 3: Solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
6a = 12 \;\Rightarrow\; a = 2, \quad 2b = -4 \;\Rightarrow\; b = -2
\end{gathered}\]
Two equations, two unknowns.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  a = 2 \) and \(\displaystyle  b = -2 \).
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 46\quad\small\texttt{(d3-046)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 46 (d3-046)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Given \( f(x) = x^3 - kx^2 + 3x \), find the value of \( k \) for which \( f''(2) = 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Differentiate twice.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 3x^2 - 2kx + 3, \quad f''(x) = 6x - 2k
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Set \( f''(2) = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
12 - 2k = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; k = 6
\end{gathered}\]
Solve for \( k \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  k = 6 \).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 47\quad\small\texttt{(d3-047)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 47 (d3-047)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 3}

\question{For \( y = 2x^3 + 5x^2 - 4x \), find the value of \( x \) at which \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Differentiate twice.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 6x^2 + 10x - 4, \quad \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 12x + 10
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Set second derivative to zero.}
\[\begin{gathered}
12x + 10 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x = -\tfrac{5}{6}
\end{gathered}\]
Solve for \( x \).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = -\dfrac{5}{6} \).
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 48\quad\small\texttt{(d3-048)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 48 (d3-048)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Given \( f(x) = x^4 - 4x^3 \), find all values of \( x \) for which \( f''(x) = 0 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Differentiate twice.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 4x^3 - 12x^2, \quad f''(x) = 12x^2 - 24x
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Solve \( f''(x) = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
12x(x - 2) = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x = 0 \text{ or } x = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Factorise.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = 0 \) or \(\displaystyle  x = 2 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 49\quad\small\texttt{(d3-049)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 49 (d3-049)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Find the stationary points of \( y = x^3 - 12x + 5 \) and use the second derivative to classify them.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 3x^2 - 12
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Set to zero.}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x^2 - 12 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x^2 = 4 \;\Rightarrow\; x = \pm 2
\end{gathered}\]
Solve for stationary points.

\textit{Step 3: Find \( y \) at each.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 2: \; 8 - 24 + 5 = -11 \\ x = -2: \; -8 + 24 + 5 = 21
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute back.

\textit{Step 4: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6x
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate once more.

\textit{Step 5: Apply the test.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\left.\tfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2}\right|_{x=2} = 12 > 0 \; (\text{min}) \\ \left.\tfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2}\right|_{x=-2} = -12 < 0 \; (\text{max})
\end{gathered}\]
A positive second derivative indicates the curve is concave up (minimum); a negative one indicates concave down (maximum). If it were exactly \(0\), we would need to examine the gradient on either side instead.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Minimum at \(\displaystyle  (2, -11) \); maximum at \(\displaystyle  (-2, 21) \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 50\quad\small\texttt{(d3-050)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 50 (d3-050)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 7}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = 2x^3 + 3x^2 - 36x + 1 \). Find its stationary points and classify them using the second derivative.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 6x^2 + 6x - 36
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Solve for stationary points.}
\[\begin{gathered}
6(x^2 + x - 6) = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; (x+3)(x-2) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Factorise.

\textit{Step 3: Find \( y \) at each.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = -3: \; -54 + 27 + 108 + 1 = 82 \\ x = 2: \; 16 + 12 - 72 + 1 = -43
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute.

\textit{Step 4: Second derivative test.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 12x + 6 \\ \text{At } x = -3: -30 < 0 \; (\text{max}) \\ \text{At } x = 2: 30 > 0 \; (\text{min})
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute each \(x\) into the second derivative. Negative \(\Rightarrow\) concave down (maximum); positive \(\Rightarrow\) concave up (minimum).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Maximum at \(\displaystyle  (-3, 82) \); minimum at \(\displaystyle  (2, -43) \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 51\quad\small\texttt{(d3-051)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 51 (d3-051)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{Find the stationary points of \( f(x) = x^3 - 6x^2 + 9x - 2 \) and classify each using \( f''(x) \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Compute \( f'(x) \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(x) = 3x^2 - 12x + 9 = 3(x-1)(x-3)
\end{gathered}\]
Factorise after taking out 3.

\textit{Step 2: Set \( f'(x) = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 1 \text{ or } x = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Two stationary points.

\textit{Step 3: Find \( y \) values.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f(1) = 1 - 6 + 9 - 2 = 2 \\ f(3) = 27 - 54 + 27 - 2 = -2
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute.

\textit{Step 4: Compute \( f''(x) \) and test.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(x) = 6x - 12 \\ f''(1) = -6 < 0 \; (\text{max}) \\ f''(3) = 6 > 0 \; (\text{min})
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the second derivative test: \(f''(x) < 0\) at a stationary point means a local maximum, \(> 0\) means a local minimum.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Maximum at \(\displaystyle  (1, 2) \); minimum at \(\displaystyle  (3, -2) \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 52\quad\small\texttt{(d3-052)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 52 (d3-052)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 8}

\question{Find the stationary points of \( y = x^4 - 4x^3 + 4x^2 + 1 \) and classify them.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Differentiate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 4x^3 - 12x^2 + 8x
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Factorise and solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
4x(x^2 - 3x + 2) = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; 4x(x-1)(x-2) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Three stationary points: \( x = 0, 1, 2 \).

\textit{Step 3: Find \( y \) at each.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y(0) = 1, \quad y(1) = 1 - 4 + 4 + 1 = 2, \quad y(2) = 16 - 32 + 16 + 1 = 1
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute.

\textit{Step 4: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 12x^2 - 24x + 8
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate once more.

\textit{Step 5: Apply the test.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x=0: 8 > 0 \; (\text{min}) \\ x=1: -4 < 0 \; (\text{max}) \\ x=2: 8 > 0 \; (\text{min})
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the second derivative test at each stationary point. Positive values give minima (concave up); negative values give a maximum (concave down).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Minima at \(\displaystyle  (0, 1) \) and \(\displaystyle  (2, 1) \); maximum at \(\displaystyle  (1, 2) \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 53\quad\small\texttt{(d3-053)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 53 (d3-053)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{For \( y = x + \dfrac{9}{x} \), \( x > 0 \), find the stationary point and use the second derivative to show that it is a minimum.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rewrite and differentiate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
y = x + 9x^{-1} \;\Rightarrow\; \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 1 - 9x^{-2}
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Set to zero.}
\[\begin{gathered}
1 - \dfrac{9}{x^2} = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x^2 = 9 \;\Rightarrow\; x = 3 \; (\text{since } x > 0)
\end{gathered}\]
Solve for \( x \).

\textit{Step 3: Find \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y(3) = 3 + 3 = 6
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute.

\textit{Step 4: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 18x^{-3} = \dfrac{18}{x^3}
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate once more.

\textit{Step 5: Evaluate at \( x = 3 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{18}{27} = \dfrac{2}{3} > 0 \; \Rightarrow \; \text{minimum}
\end{gathered}\]
The second derivative is positive at the stationary point, so the curve is concave up there. This confirms a local minimum.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Stationary point at \(\displaystyle  (3, 6) \); it is a minimum.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 54\quad\small\texttt{(d3-054)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 54 (d3-054)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = x^3 - 3x \). (a) Find the stationary points. (b) Classify each using the second derivative.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 3x^2 - 3
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Solve.}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x^2 - 3 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x^2 = 1 \;\Rightarrow\; x = \pm 1
\end{gathered}\]
Two stationary points.

\textit{Step 3: Find \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y(1) = -2, \quad y(-1) = 2
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute.

\textit{Step 4: (b) Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6x \\ x = 1: 6 > 0 \; (\text{min}) \\ x = -1: -6 < 0 \; (\text{max})
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute each \(x\) into the second derivative. Positive value \(\Rightarrow\) concave up (minimum); negative value \(\Rightarrow\) concave down (maximum).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  (1, -2) \) and \(\displaystyle  (-1, 2) \) \\[3pt]  (b) \(\displaystyle  (1, -2) \) minimum, \(\displaystyle  (-1, 2) \) maximum.
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 55\quad\small\texttt{(d3-055)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 55 (d3-055)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the range of values of \( x \) for which the curve \( y = x^3 - 6x^2 + 5 \) is concave up.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Differentiate twice.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 3x^2 - 12x, \quad \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6x - 12
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Concave up when second derivative is positive.}
\[\begin{gathered}
6x - 12 > 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x > 2
\end{gathered}\]
A curve is concave up where the second derivative is positive --- this is where the gradient is increasing. Solve the linear inequality to find the interval.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Concave up for \(\displaystyle  x > 2 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 56\quad\small\texttt{(d3-056)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 56 (d3-056)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Find the \( x \)-coordinate of the point of inflection of \( y = x^3 - 9x^2 + 24x - 15 \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6x - 18
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate twice.

\textit{Step 2: Set to zero.}
\[\begin{gathered}
6x - 18 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Candidate for inflection.

\textit{Step 3: Check sign change.}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(2) = -6 < 0, \; f''(4) = 6 > 0
\end{gathered}\]
For a genuine point of inflection the second derivative must change sign across the candidate \(x\)-value. Here it goes from negative to positive, so concavity flips and this is indeed an inflection point.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Point of inflection at \(\displaystyle  x = 3 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 57\quad\small\texttt{(d3-057)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 57 (d3-057)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{A curve has equation \( y = x^4 - 6x^2 \). Find the range of values of \( x \) for which the curve is concave down.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Differentiate twice.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 4x^3 - 12x, \quad \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 12x^2 - 12
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Concave down when second derivative is negative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
12x^2 - 12 < 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x^2 < 1 \;\Rightarrow\; -1 < x < 1
\end{gathered}\]
A curve is concave down where its second derivative is negative. Be careful taking the square root of an inequality: \(x^2 < 1\) means \(|x| < 1\), giving the interval \(-1 < x < 1\).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Concave down on \(\displaystyle  -1 < x < 1 \).
\end{answerbox}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 58\quad\small\texttt{(d3-058)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 58 (d3-058)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The curve \( y = 2x^3 - 9x^2 + 12x \) has a point of inflection. Find its coordinates.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 12x - 18
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate twice.

\textit{Step 2: Set to zero.}
\[\begin{gathered}
12x - 18 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x = \tfrac{3}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Candidate for inflection.

\textit{Step 3: Find \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y\!\left(\tfrac{3}{2}\right) = 2 \cdot \tfrac{27}{8} - 9 \cdot \tfrac{9}{4} + 12 \cdot \tfrac{3}{2} = \tfrac{27}{4} - \tfrac{81}{4} + 18 = -\tfrac{54}{4} + 18 = \tfrac{9}{2}
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Point of inflection at \(\displaystyle  \left(\tfrac{3}{2},\, \tfrac{9}{2}\right) \).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\medskip\hrule

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 59\quad\small\texttt{(d3-059)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 59 (d3-059)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{Find the values of \( x \) for which the curve \( y = x^3 - 3x^2 \) is decreasing and concave up.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: First derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 3x^2 - 6x = 3x(x-2)
\end{gathered}\]
Factorise.

\textit{Step 2: Decreasing when this is negative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
3x(x-2) < 0 \;\Rightarrow\; 0 < x < 2
\end{gathered}\]
Quadratic is negative between roots.

\textit{Step 3: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6x - 6
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate again.

\textit{Step 4: Concave up when this is positive.}
\[\begin{gathered}
6x - 6 > 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x > 1
\end{gathered}\]
Solve.

\textit{Step 5: Intersect the two conditions.}
\[\begin{gathered}
(0 < x < 2) \cap (x > 1) \;\Rightarrow\; 1 < x < 2
\end{gathered}\]
Both conditions hold here.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad Decreasing and concave up on \(\displaystyle  1 < x < 2 \).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 60\quad\small\texttt{(d3-060)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 60 (d3-060)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 4}

\question{Show that the curve \( y = x^4 + 2 \) has no point of inflection.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 12x^2
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate twice.

\textit{Step 2: Set to zero.}
\[\begin{gathered}
12x^2 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Candidate point.

\textit{Step 3: Check the sign of the second derivative around \( x = 0 \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f''(-1) = 12 > 0, \quad f''(1) = 12 > 0
\end{gathered}\]
The second derivative does not change sign across \( x = 0 \).

\textit{Step 4: Conclusion.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{No point of inflection at } x = 0 \;\checkmark
\end{gathered}\]
A point of inflection requires \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} \) to change sign across the point. Here \(12x^2 \geq 0\) for all \(x\), so it never changes sign --- there is no inflection point anywhere on the curve.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad No point of inflection --- \(\displaystyle  f''(x) = 12x^2 \geq 0 \) everywhere.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 61\quad\small\texttt{(d3-061)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 61 (d3-061)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{A rectangular enclosure has perimeter 20 m. Its area \( A \) (in m\textsuperscript{2}) is given by \( A = x(10 - x) \), where \( x \) is the length of one side. Find the value of \( x \) that maximises \( A \), and use the second derivative to justify that it is a maximum.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Expand.}
\[\begin{gathered}
A = 10x - x^2
\end{gathered}\]
Remove the brackets.

\textit{Step 2: Differentiate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}A}{\mathrm{d}x} = 10 - 2x
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 3: Set to zero.}
\[\begin{gathered}
10 - 2x = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x = 5
\end{gathered}\]
Solve.

\textit{Step 4: Second derivative test.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2A}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = -2 < 0 \;\Rightarrow\; \text{maximum}
\end{gathered}\]
The second derivative is the constant \(-2\), so the area function is concave down everywhere. This confirms the stationary point is a maximum.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = 5 \) m gives the maximum area (a square of side 5 m).
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 62\quad\small\texttt{(d3-062)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 62 (d3-062)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 6}

\question{The height of a projectile \( h \) metres after \( t \) seconds is \( h = 25t - 5t^2 \). (a) Find \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}h}{\mathrm{d}t} \) and \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2h}{\mathrm{d}t^2} \). (b) Find the time at which the height is maximum and state the maximum height. (c) Interpret the sign of \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2h}{\mathrm{d}t^2} \).}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Differentiate twice.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}h}{\mathrm{d}t} = 25 - 10t, \quad \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2h}{\mathrm{d}t^2} = -10
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 2: (b) Maximum when velocity is zero.}
\[\begin{gathered}
25 - 10t = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; t = 2.5
\end{gathered}\]
Solve.

\textit{Step 3: Maximum height.}
\[\begin{gathered}
h(2.5) = 62.5 - 31.25 = 31.25 \text{ m}
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute.

\textit{Step 4: (c) Interpretation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2h}{\mathrm{d}t^2} = -10 \text{ m/s}^2
\end{gathered}\]
The second derivative of position with respect to time is acceleration. A constant \(-10\) m/s\textsuperscript{2} is gravity acting downwards, and its negative sign also tells us the height function is concave down --- consistent with the projectile turning around at a maximum.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) \(\displaystyle  \tfrac{\mathrm{d}h}{\mathrm{d}t} = 25 - 10t \), \(\displaystyle  \tfrac{\mathrm{d}^2h}{\mathrm{d}t^2} = -10 \) \\[3pt]  (b) \(\displaystyle  t = 2.5 \) s, max height 31.25 m \\[3pt]  (c) Acceleration is \(\displaystyle  -10 \) m/s\textsuperscript{2} (downward).
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\begin{center}
\resizebox{\ifdim\width>\linewidth \linewidth\else \width\fi}{!}{%
\begin{tikzpicture}[>={Stealth[length=2mm]},font=\footnotesize,line cap=round,line join=round]
\begin{scope}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 63\quad\small\texttt{(d3-063)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 63 (d3-063)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 8}

\question{An open-top box is made from a square sheet of side 12 cm by cutting a square of side \( x \) cm from each corner and folding up the sides. The volume is \( V = x(12 - 2x)^2 \) cm\textsuperscript{3}. Find the value of \( x \) that maximises the volume and verify it is a maximum using the second derivative.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Expand.}
\[\begin{gathered}
V = x(144 - 48x + 4x^2) = 4x^3 - 48x^2 + 144x
\end{gathered}\]
Multiply out.

\textit{Step 2: Differentiate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}V}{\mathrm{d}x} = 12x^2 - 96x + 144
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 3: Set to zero.}
\[\begin{gathered}
12(x^2 - 8x + 12) = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; (x-2)(x-6) = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Factorise.

\textit{Step 4: Valid solution.}
\[\begin{gathered}
x = 2 \; (\text{since } 0 < x < 6)
\end{gathered}\]
For a real box, \( 12 - 2x > 0 \), so \( x < 6 \).

\textit{Step 5: Second derivative test.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2V}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 24x - 96 \\ \left.\tfrac{\mathrm{d}^2V}{\mathrm{d}x^2}\right|_{x=2} = 48 - 96 = -48 < 0
\end{gathered}\]
A negative second derivative at the stationary point means the volume function is concave down there, confirming \(x = 2\) gives a maximum (not a minimum).

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  x = 2 \) cm gives the maximum volume; \(\displaystyle  V(2) = 128 \) cm\textsuperscript{3}.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 64\quad\small\texttt{(d3-064)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 64 (d3-064)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 5}

\question{The population \( P \) (in thousands) of a species \( t \) years after a study begins is modelled by \( P = 20 + 6t - t^2 \). (a) Find the time at which the population is greatest and its value. (b) State the sign of \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2P}{\mathrm{d}t^2} \) and interpret it.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Differentiate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}P}{\mathrm{d}t} = 6 - 2t
\end{gathered}\]
Rate of change of population.

\textit{Step 2: Set to zero.}
\[\begin{gathered}
6 - 2t = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; t = 3
\end{gathered}\]
Critical time.

\textit{Step 3: Maximum population.}
\[\begin{gathered}
P(3) = 20 + 18 - 9 = 29
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute.

\textit{Step 4: (b) Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2P}{\mathrm{d}t^2} = -2 < 0
\end{gathered}\]
The second derivative is a constant \(-2\), negative throughout. This means the rate of change of \(P\) (the growth rate) is itself always decreasing.

\textit{Step 5: Interpretation.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\text{Rate of growth is always decreasing.}
\end{gathered}\]
The population grows more slowly over time, then declines.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) Maximum population is 29 thousand at \(\displaystyle  t = 3 \) years. \\[3pt]  (b) \(\displaystyle  \tfrac{\mathrm{d}^2P}{\mathrm{d}t^2} = -2 \), the growth rate is always decreasing.
\end{answerbox}
\textbf{Diagram.}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 65\quad\small\texttt{(d3-065)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 65 (d3-065)}
\textit{Difficulty: Standard \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 7}

\question{A cylinder has surface area \( S = 2\pi r^2 + \dfrac{200}{r} \) for \( r > 0 \). Find the value of \( r \) that minimises \( S \), and use the second derivative to confirm it is a minimum.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: Rewrite and differentiate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
S = 2\pi r^2 + 200 r^{-1} \;\Rightarrow\; \dfrac{\mathrm{d}S}{\mathrm{d}r} = 4\pi r - 200 r^{-2}
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Set to zero.}
\[\begin{gathered}
4\pi r = \dfrac{200}{r^2} \;\Rightarrow\; r^3 = \dfrac{50}{\pi} \;\Rightarrow\; r = \sqrt[3]{\dfrac{50}{\pi}}
\end{gathered}\]
Rearrange.

\textit{Step 3: Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2S}{\mathrm{d}r^2} = 4\pi + 400 r^{-3} = 4\pi + \dfrac{400}{r^3}
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate once more.

\textit{Step 4: Confirm minimum.}
\[\begin{gathered}
r > 0 \;\Rightarrow\; \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2S}{\mathrm{d}r^2} > 0
\end{gathered}\]
Both \(4\pi\) and \(\dfrac{400}{r^3}\) are positive when \(r > 0\), so the second derivative is strictly positive on the domain. Concave up at the stationary point means a local minimum.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad \(\displaystyle  r = \sqrt[3]{\dfrac{50}{\pi}} \) gives the minimum surface area.
\end{answerbox}

\section*{Second Order Derivatives 66\quad\small\texttt{(d3-066)}}
\addcontentsline{toc}{section}{Second Order Derivatives 66 (d3-066)}
\textit{Difficulty: Challenge \quad\textbar\quad Marks: 8}

\question{The curve \( C \) has equation \( y = x^3 - 3ax^2 + 3a^2 x \), where \( a \) is a positive constant. (a) Find the stationary points of \( C \) in terms of \( a \). (b) Find the nature of each stationary point using the second derivative.}

\textbf{Worked solution.}

\textit{Step 1: (a) Differentiate.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 3x^2 - 6ax + 3a^2
\end{gathered}\]
Apply the power rule.

\textit{Step 2: Set to zero and simplify.}
\[\begin{gathered}
3(x^2 - 2ax + a^2) = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; (x - a)^2 = 0 \;\Rightarrow\; x = a
\end{gathered}\]
Repeated root.

\textit{Step 3: Find \( y \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
y(a) = a^3 - 3a \cdot a^2 + 3a^2 \cdot a = a^3
\end{gathered}\]
Substitute.

\textit{Step 4: (b) Second derivative.}
\[\begin{gathered}
\dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 6x - 6a
\end{gathered}\]
Differentiate once more.

\textit{Step 5: Evaluate at \( x = a \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
6a - 6a = 0
\end{gathered}\]
Second derivative test is inconclusive.

\textit{Step 6: Check sign change around \( x = a \).}
\[\begin{gathered}
f'(a - \epsilon) > 0, \quad f'(a + \epsilon) > 0
\end{gathered}\]
When \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}^2y}{\mathrm{d}x^2} = 0 \) the second-derivative test is inconclusive. Falling back on first-derivative behaviour: \( \dfrac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}x} = 3(x-a)^2 \geq 0 \) for all \(x\), so the gradient does not change sign --- this is a stationary point of inflection, not a turning point.

\begin{answerbox}
\textbf{Final answer:}\quad (a) One stationary point at \(\displaystyle  (a, a^3) \). \\[3pt]  (b) It is a stationary point of inflection.
\end{answerbox}
